const options = {
  method: 'POST',
  headers: {
    Authorization: 'Bearer fc-key',
    'Content-Type': 'application/json'
  },
  body: JSON.stringify({
    "query": "Busan, South Korea within downtown: official public transport authority, operator and airport fare pages covering adult local single tickets, useful day tickets or contactless caps, and main airport-to-city public transport. Prioritise original-currency fares, zones, validity, transfer windows, peak/off-peak rules, payment/validation instructions and airport exclusions. Include local-language official sources. Distinguish tickets from automatic caps and different airports/operators. Exclude forums and generic travel-budget estimates.",
    "sources": [
        "web",
        "news"
    ],
    "categories": [],
    "limit": 15,
    "scrapeOptions": {
        "onlyMainContent": true,
        "maxAge": 2505600000,
        "parsers": [
            "pdf"
        ],
        "formats": [
            "markdown",
            "summary"
        ]
      }
})
};

try {
  const response = await fetch(url, options);
  const data = await response.json();
  console.log(data);
} catch (error) {

        "position": 1,
        "markdown": "![Seoul subway station with transit card users and modern transit infrastructure](https://cdn.korvia.com/korvia.com/magazine/korea-transit-hero.webp)\n\nPractical Guide\n\n# Korea Public Transportation Guide for Expats (2026)\n\nUpdated: May 4, 2026 · 15 min read\n\nKorea's public transportation system is one of the most reliable, efficient, and affordable in the world. Whether you're an English teacher arriving for your first contract, an expat relocating for work, or a job applicant exploring your options, mastering transit in Korea unlocks independence and saves you thousands of won per month compared to taxis and ride-hailing alone.\n\nThis guide walks you through every major transit method in Korea — from the Seoul subway to KTX high-speed trains, from Kakao T taxis to AREX airport trains. We'll cover practical payments (T-money, Apple Pay), navigation apps (Naver Map vs Kakao Map), foreigner-specific passes, accessibility, and real cost breakdowns so you can budget and plan with confidence.\n\n## The 5-Minute Starter Pack: Three Things You Need Today\n\nIf you're arriving in Korea this week, here's the absolute minimum:\n\n1. **T-money card.** Buy one at any convenience store (GS25, CU, 7-Eleven, or Emart24). Cost: ₩2,500–₩4,000 card fee, plus your first charge (usually ₩10,000–₩20,000). This single card works on subways, buses, taxis, and even convenience store payments nationwide.\n2. **Naver Map app.** Download it before your flight. It's the gold standard for public transit routing in Korea with real-time subway and bus schedules, English support, and integration with Naver payments.\n3. **Kakao T app.** This is Korea's primary ride-hailing app for taxis. Download it, register your phone number (you can use a temporary Korean prepaid SIM), and you can book rides from day one.\n\nThat's your foundation. Everything else in this guide is optimization.\n\n## T-Money Card: Comprehensive Guide to Korea's Universal Transit Pass\n\n### Where to buy and how to set up\n\nT-money cards are ubiquitous. Walk into any convenience store and ask for \"T-money\" (the staff will understand). You'll pay a one-time card fee (₩2,500–₩4,000 depending on the card design) and then add your first balance in cash or, as of March 2026, with a foreign credit card at supported kiosks.\n\nOnce activated, your T-money card becomes your gateway to the entire transit ecosystem: subways, city buses, express buses, airport limousine buses, many taxis, and hundreds of convenience stores nationwide.\n\n### Topping up your card: Cash, foreign cards (March 2026 update), and apps\n\nFor years, topping up T-money required either cash at kiosks or a Korean bank account. As of March 2026, this changed significantly.\n\n| Method | Availability | Minimum Charge |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Cash at subway kiosks | Nationwide (ubiquitous) | Any amount (typically ₩10,000+) |\n| Foreign credit card (Visa/MC/Amex) | Major cities (Seoul, Busan, Daegu, etc.) | ₩10,000–₩100,000 |\n| Convenience store checkout | Most convenient stores nationwide | ₩1,000–₩100,000 |\n| T-money mobile app | iOS/Android (requires Korean bank or card) | Any amount |\n| Kakao Pay / Naver Pay | Most kiosks and convenience stores | ₩1,000–₩100,000 |\n\nKorvia Tip\n\nThe foreign credit card kiosk acceptance varies by station and city, but it's rapidly expanding. Always test your card at your local subway station's kiosk in your first week. If it fails, keep cash on hand or ask your co-teacher where the nearest convenience store with accepted payment is.\n\n### T-money pricing and transfer discounts\n\nSingle rides cost around **₩1,400–₩1,500** for subways and buses depending on distance. The real magic is the **30-minute transfer discount**: if you tap your T-money card again on a different bus or subway within 30 minutes of your first tap, the second ride is either free or deeply discounted.\n\nThis transfer window works across transit types too, so you can take a subway and connect to a bus (or vice versa) and still qualify for the discount. Plan your route accordingly and you'll rarely pay full fare twice.\n\n### Refunding your T-money card\n\nBefore you leave Korea, your T-money card balance can be refunded. Visit a convenience store and ask for \"T-money 환불\" (refund). They will process a refund to cash, though the card fee is usually not returned. Keep your receipt and the card itself to complete the refund.\n\n## Seoul Subway: System Overview, English Navigation, and Avoiding Peak Hours\n\n### Line structure and major stations\n\nSeoul has 9 main metro lines plus 8 commuter lines, color-coded and numbered for clarity. English signage is comprehensive, with both Romanized station names and English translations on all platforms. Major hub stations (Gangnam, Seoul Station, Jongno 3-ga, Insadong) have English-speaking staff and information booths.\n\nFor teachers living outside Seoul, commuter lines (Bundang Line, Shinbundang Line, Incheon Metro) extend service to bedroom communities where much of the affordable housing is located. Your Naver Map app will show exact transfer points and which line to take.\n\n### Transfers and connection safety\n\nSeoul's subway is engineered for transfers. Following color-coded signs and the English signage, you can navigate between any two lines in under 5 minutes at well-designed stations. Stations like Jongno 3-ga (serving 5 lines) can be more complex, but even there, the signage is logical.\n\nFor security, stations are well-lit and monitored by CCTV. Women's-only cars (typically the first or last car, marked with a pink line) are common and safe. Avoid traveling alone very late at night (after midnight) in less-busy outer stations, though the system is generally very safe compared to Western cities.\n\n### Peak hours and scheduling strategy\n\n**Rush hour** is 7:30–9:00 am and 5:30–7:30 pm on weekdays. Trains are packed shoulder-to-shoulder during these windows. If your schedule allows, avoid peak hours by arriving at work 30 minutes earlier or leaving 30 minutes later.\n\nMost teachers don't have this luxury, so adjust your expectations: you will experience crowded trains. The silver lining is frequency — trains run every 3–5 minutes during rush hour, so even if one is too packed, another arrives within seconds. Weekends are much calmer, running every 10–15 minutes.\n\nKorvia Tip\n\nIf you live close enough to walk or bike to school, you'll save hours per month in commuting. Many EPIK placements intentionally house teachers within walking distance of their schools. Ask your placement coordinator about the commute distance before you arrive.\n\n## Navigation Apps: Naver Map vs Kakao Map (English Support and Real-Time Routing)\n\n### Naver Map: The transit expert's choice\n\nNaver Map is owned by Naver, Korea's dominant search company, which gives it access to real-time transit data directly from Seoul Metro and bus operators. The English interface is comprehensive and includes detailed arrival times, platform numbers, and exit recommendations.\n\nSearch for your destination, select the \"Public Transport\" tab, and you get a ranked list of routes by travel time with exact fare and transfer points. It integrates with Naver Pay for direct booking on some services, though you'll primarily use it for planning.\n\n### Kakao Map: Intuitive and taxi-integrated\n\nKakao Map is slightly more intuitive for casual users and has a critical advantage: native integration with Kakao T (Korea's primary taxi app). While Naver Map shows transit routes well, Kakao Map allows you to seamlessly switch between \"I want to take a bus/subway\" and \"I want to book a taxi right now\" without leaving the app.\n\nEnglish support on Kakao Map is good but slightly less comprehensive than Naver Map. For transit planning, Naver is marginally better; for overall mobility including taxis, Kakao is more convenient.\n\n### Practical advice: Use both\n\nMost experienced expats in Korea install both apps. Use Naver Map for detailed transit routing and Kakao Map for taxi integration and quick re-routing if you're running late or conditions change. The slight redundancy is worth the flexibility.\n\n## Bus System: City Buses, Express Buses, and Airport Limousine Buses\n\n### City buses vs express buses: Speed, comfort, and cost\n\nSeoul and major cities have two categories of buses: regular city buses (stopping every 2–3 blocks, same price as subway, around ₩1,400–₩1,500) and express buses (faster routes with fewer stops, slightly higher fare around ₩2,100–₩2,500).\n\nCity buses are your daily workhorses — they cover neighborhoods that the subway doesn't reach. Express buses are useful for longer cross-city trips where saving 15–20 minutes is worth the extra ₩1,000. Your Naver Map or Kakao Map app will show both options with time and cost.\n\n### Airport limousine buses\n\nFor trips from central Seoul to Incheon or Gimpo airports, limousine buses are the overlooked hero: they cost ₩6,000–₩10,000, run every 15–30 minutes, and include luggage space (unlike the subway or taxis). They're less exciting than the AREX express train, but often more convenient if your hotel or apartment is near a pick-up stop.\n\nLimousine bus stops are marked clearly at hotels, shopping centers, and apartment complexes. You can book online or pay the driver directly.\n\n## Taxi Apps: Kakao T, k.ride, Uber, and Traditional Flag-Down (April 2025 Update)\n\n### Kakao T: The dominant choice\n\nKakao T is used for 80%+ of app-booked taxi rides in Korea. Fares are metered and transparent before you book, starting at **₩3,800–₩4,800** for a basic ride, with surge pricing during rush hours and late nights (1.2x–1.5x multiplier common). All drivers are registered taxis, background-checked, and rated.\n\nThe app works with just a Korean phone number. You can request a ride in seconds, track the driver in real-time, and pay by card or cash (both are standard). Receipts are automatic, useful for expense reports if needed.\n\n### k.ride: New foreigner-friendly alternative (April 2025)\n\nk.ride launched in April 2025 as an alternative ride-hailing platform explicitly designed for international users. English language support is native (not translated), and registration accepts international phone numbers without requiring a Korean bank account or ARC.\n\nWhile k.ride's driver network is smaller than Kakao T, it's rapidly expanding in Seoul and major cities. For expats arriving without full Korean infrastructure set up yet, k.ride can be a useful bridge until you open a Korean bank account and set up Kakao T payments fully.\n\n### Uber: Limited presence\n\nUber operates in select cities (Incheon, Jeju) but is not available in Seoul or most major Korean cities. Kakao T and local competitors have effectively dominated the market. Don't plan on Uber as a primary option.\n\n### Traditional flag-down taxis\n\nYou can always hail a taxi on the street by raising your hand. Fares are metered and cheaper than app-booked rides (no platform fee), starting at ₩3,000 for the initial drop. However, communication can be difficult if the driver doesn't speak English. Most drivers have a laminated English phrase sheet, but typing your destination into their GPS or showing them a map on your phone is faster.\n\nKorvia Tip\n\nTaxis are expensive compared to public transit. A subway ride costs ₩1,500, but a taxi for the same distance might cost ₩8,000–₩12,000. Use taxis for emergencies, late-night returns (safer than walking alone), or when carrying heavy luggage. For daily commuting, the T-money subway/bus system is dramatically more economical.\n\n## KTX and High-Speed Trains: Booking, Routes, and the KORAIL Pass\n\n### What is KTX and why you'll use it\n\nThe KTX (Korea Train eXpress) is Korea's high-speed rail network, connecting Seoul to Busan (2h 40m), Daegu (2h), Daejeon (1h 10m), and dozens of other cities. Trains run every 15–30 minutes during the day, with comfortable seats, power outlets, and a small café car. Fares range from ₩25,000 to ₩65,000 depending on distance and seat class (Standard vs Premium).\n\nFor English teachers, the KTX unlocks weekend travel without flying. A Friday evening train to Busan gets you there by 9 pm; Sunday evening you're back for work Monday. Many teachers use the KTX monthly for budget travel across the country.\n\n### Booking in English via KORAIL\n\nKORAIL's official English website [korail.com](https://www.korail.com/global/eng) allows direct booking with your passport number or foreigner ID. You pay with an international credit card, and the site issues a reference number. Pick up physical tickets at any station counter using your reference number and passport, or print mobile tickets directly (preferred — no queue).\n\nBooking opens 30 days in advance. Peak times (Friday evening, Sunday afternoon) sell out fast, so book early if you want a specific time.\n\n### The KORAIL Pass: Worth it for frequent travelers\n\nIf you plan more than 3–4 KTX trips per month, the KORAIL Pass (₩65,000–₩170,000 for 3–7 days of unlimited travel) can save money. However, for occasional weekend trips, individual tickets are cheaper. Purchase the pass through korail.com and activate it only when you're ready to use it (it has an expiration date).\n\n## Incheon Airport Transfers: AREX Express vs All-Stop vs Limousine Bus\n\n### AREX Express: Fast but pricey\n\nThe AREX (Airport Railroad) express train gets you from Incheon Airport Terminal 1 to Seoul Station in 43 minutes. The fare is ₩9,000 for express (no stops) or ₩4,750 for the all-stop version (44 minutes to Seoul Station). Your T-money card works on both.\n\nAREX is best if you're staying in central Seoul (near Seoul Station) and want speed without negotiating taxis. However, if your final destination is Gangnam or Bundang (south or east Seoul), you'll spend another ₩1,500–₩3,000 and 20+ minutes on the subway after reaching Seoul Station.\n\n### AREX all-stop train: Cheaper and more flexible\n\nThe all-stop version stops at intermediate stations (Gimpo, Hongdae, Yeongdeungpo, Incheon Station). If your destination is one of these stops, you save significantly. At ₩4,750, it's cheaper than any taxi and gives you flexibility for layovers or shopping before heading onward.\n\n### Limousine bus: Luggage-friendly alternative\n\nA dedicated limousine bus (₩6,000–₩10,000) takes you directly to most major hotels, business districts, and subway stations. If you have a lot of luggage or your destination is off the subway grid, the limousine bus is less stressful than coordinating two transit changes.\n\n### Practical advice: Avoid taxis from the airport\n\nAirport taxis are metered, but the starting fare from Incheon is around ₩11,000–₩13,000, plus an additional ₩20,000+ to reach central Seoul (total ₩31,000–₩35,000). This is 3–4x the AREX express cost. Save taxis for emergencies or late-night arrivals when trains stop running (they run until ~11:50 pm).\n\n## Beyond Seoul: Busan, Daegu, Daejeon Metro Systems and Regional Differences\n\n### Busan Metro: Smaller but comprehensive\n\nBusan has 4 metro lines serving the port city and surrounding suburbs. The system is smaller and simpler than Seoul but equally clean and modern. T-money cards work here too. Fare structure is the same: single rides ₩1,400–₩1,500, with transfer discounts within 30 minutes.\n\n### Daegu, Daejeon, and other cities\n\nDaegu has 2 metro lines, Daejeon has 1, and Incheon has 1. All use T-money cards with similar pricing. Beyond these, most Korean cities rely on bus networks rather than underground metro. For non-Seoul cities, combine the KTX for intercity travel with local buses and T-money for in-city transit.\n\n### Regional quirks: Cash-heavy communities\n\nOutside Seoul, some smaller cities and rural areas have lower digital payment adoption for buses. Always keep ₩10,000–₩20,000 in cash when traveling to smaller cities, in case a specific bus route or station doesn't accept T-money or cards.\n\n## Foreigner-Specific Cards and Passes: WOWPASS, Mpass, and Climate Card\n\n### WOWPASS: Tourism-focused but low value for residents\n\nThe WOWPASS is a rechargeable card marketed to tourists and short-term visitors. It works on Seoul transit the same as T-money, but offers minimal discounts and requires purchasing at higher minimums (₩20,000+). For teachers and expats staying 6+ months, a regular T-money card is better value.\n\n### Mpass (M-pass): For monthly unlimited commuters\n\nThe M-pass is a monthly pass offering unlimited unlimited subway rides within a specified zone for around ₩50,000–₩75,000 depending on coverage area. If your daily commute costs ₩1,500 and you commute 20 days/month, regular T-money costs ₩30,000. The M-pass breakeven is around 33+ rides per month.\n\nM-pass is valuable if you have a long commute (e.g., outer Bundang Line) and use transit daily. For shorter commutes or less frequent use, individual T-money rides are cheaper. Calculate your exact usage before committing.\n\n### Climate Card: Environmental incentive (low adoption)\n\nKorea has experimented with a \"Climate Card\" that rewards eco-friendly transit use with discounts. As of 2026, adoption is still limited and offers are minimal. Most expats don't see practical value yet. Stick with T-money or M-pass for now.\n\n## Apple Pay and Contactless Transit: April 2026 Mastercard Support Update\n\n### Apple Pay expansion for transit: Mastercard now supported\n\nAs of April 2026, Apple Pay in Korea expanded beyond Visa to support Mastercard for transit payments. This is a significant change, as most travelers and expats hold Visa or Mastercard (not domestic Korean cards).\n\nTo use Apple Pay for transit in Korea: add a Mastercard or Visa credit card to Apple Wallet, then tap your iPhone or Apple Watch on compatible readers at subway gates and bus payment terminals. Your card will be charged instantly.\n\n### Coverage: Growing but not complete\n\nApple Pay transit coverage is currently strongest in Seoul and expands to Busan, Daegu, and major cities. Smaller cities and rural areas are not yet covered. Combined with the T-money card, Apple Pay is a convenient backup for daily commutes in cities where it's available, but not a replacement.\n\n### Practical use: Convenience over savings\n\nApple Pay and T-money have identical fares — the advantage of Apple Pay is speed and not carrying a physical card. However, if you forget your iPhone or the battery dies, you're stuck. For reliability, keep your T-money card as your primary method and use Apple Pay as a convenience option when you have your phone.\n\nKorvia Tip\n\nMany subways and buses in Korea have contactless readers that accept both NFC (Apple Pay, Android Pay) and RFID (T-money cards). Test your payment method at your local subway station before relying on it during rush hour.\n\n## Accessibility: Wheelchair Access, Visual and Hearing Impairment Support, and English Assistance\n\n### Wheelchair accessibility: Subways and buses\n\nSeoul Metro publishes detailed accessibility information for each station, including elevator locations, platform widths, and boarding assistance. Not all stations are fully accessible — older stations have limited elevator infrastructure — but most main lines (Lines 1–4) have comprehensive accessibility. The Seoul Metro website [has an accessibility guide in English](https://english.seoul.go.kr/service/movement/public-transportation/).\n\nBuses gradually implement ramps and wheelchair spaces, though coverage is less universal than subways. Call the transit operator ahead of time if you need specific accessibility information about a route.\n\n### Visual and hearing impairment support\n\nSeoul metro platforms have tactile paving (bumps) leading to ticket gates and elevator buttons have Braille labels. Audio announcements in Korean and English play at each station, and LED signs display upcoming stations. Station staff at major hubs are trained to assist visually or hearing-impaired passengers.\n\n### English-speaking assistance hotlines\n\nSeoul Metro operates an English-language information line: +82-2-6319-9300. KTX customer service: +82-1544-7788 (some English support). Most major stations and airports have English-speaking info booths. Your school's administrative office can usually help translate if you encounter a transit issue.\n\n## Monthly Transit Budget for English Teachers: Cost Breakdown\n\nFor a typical English teacher in Seoul or a provincial city:\n\n| Category | Cost/Month | Notes |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Daily subway/bus commute | ₩30,000–₩50,000 | 20–30 work days × ₩1,500 per ride |\n| Monthly weekend trips | ₩10,000–₩20,000 | 1–2 short local bus/subway trips |\n| Intercity travel (1–2 KTX trips) | ₩50,000–₩100,000 | KTX weekend getaways |\n| Occasional taxis (emergencies/late-night) | ₩10,000–₩30,000 | 2–4 taxi rides for special cases |\n| Total Monthly | ₩100,000–₩200,000 | Most teachers fall in this range |\n\nThis is dramatically lower than car ownership (insurance, gas, parking) or regular taxi use. For context, a teacher earning ₩2,000,000–₩2,500,000 per month spends 5–10% of income on transit, far below Western norms.\n\n## Pro Tips and Common Mistakes to Avoid\n\n- **Don't buy your T-money card at the airport.** Airport convenience stores charge more. Wait until you reach your accommodation and buy it locally.\n- **Set phone alarms for your transit transfers.** The 30-minute transfer window is strict. Get off at your stop, and if you're not close to your next transit connection, you might miss the discount.\n- **Avoid eating or drinking on subways (except water).** This is a cultural norm in Korea — you'll draw stares.\n- **Give up your seat to elderly passengers, children, and pregnant women.** This is expected, not optional. Priority seating areas have red signage.\n- **Don't book Kakao T during rush hour if you can walk.** Surge pricing multipliers (1.5x–2x) during peak hours make taxis expensive. Subways and buses are never more expensive.\n- **Use KakaoMap offline download for neighborhoods.** While you're usually connected, downloading offline maps of your school neighborhood helps you navigate if data is slow.\n- **Don't count on trains and buses after midnight.** Most public transit stops running by 11:30 pm–12:30 am. Plan your late-night returns as taxis or stay over with a friend.\n- **Save your KORAIL booking reference numbers.** If you miss a train, you can rebook to a later train the same day at most stations.\n\nKorvia Tip\n\nDownload both Naver Map and Kakao Map before your flight, even with offline maps enabled. When you land and get a Korean SIM, you'll be able to plan your first route to your accommodation without burning through data or paper maps.\n\n## Bottom Line: Mastering Korea's Transit System in Your First Month\n\nKorea's public transportation is reliable, affordable, and designed for daily use. Your first week in Korea, your priorities are:\n\n1. Buy a T-money card at a convenience store (₩2,500–₩4,000 card fee, ₩10,000–₩20,000 first charge).\n2. Download Naver Map and Kakao Map on your phone (before your flight if possible).\n3. Download Kakao T to have ride-hailing available from day one.\n4. Test your card at your local subway station's ticket gate.\n\nBy the end of week 1, you'll have solved 90% of your transit needs for the entire year. From there, weekend KTX trips, navigating Busan or Daegu, and optimizing your commute are all incremental skills you'll develop naturally.\n\nReady to teach in Korea and experience this transit system firsthand? [Apply through Korvia](https://www.korvia.com/apply), and we'll place you in a school where you can walk to your apartment or reach it in under 45 minutes on reliable public transit.\n\n## Related Articles\n\n[Guide **Cost of Living in Korea as an English Teacher (2026 Budget Guide)**](https://www.korvia.com/magazine/cost-of-living-korea-english-teacher-2026) [Guide **First Month Survival Guide — Teaching in Korea (Week 1-4)**](https://www.korvia.com/magazine/first-month-survival-korea-teacher) [Guide **Hagwon vs EPIK in 2027: Which Is Right for You?**](https://www.korvia.com/magazine/hagwon-vs-epik-korea-2026) [Guide **Which TEFL Certificate Do You Need for Korea? (2026 Guide)**](https://www.korvia.com/magazine/tefl-certificate-korea-guide-2026)\n\n## Ready to Navigate Korea Like a Local?\n\nApply today to teach English in Korea and master its world-class transit system.\n\n[Apply Now](https://www.korvia.com/apply) [More Articles](https://www.korvia.com/magazine)\n\n![Korvia](https://cdn.korvia.com/korvia.com/app-media/20260806-r2-only/favicon-96x96.png-w96.webp)\n\n## Cookie settings\n\nKorvia uses optional cookies for site analytics (Microsoft Clarity, Zoho SalesIQ) and ad measurement (Google, Microsoft). They stay off unless you allow them; essential cookies only keep the site working.         "summary": "The \"Korea Public Transportation Guide for Expats\" provides comprehensive information on navigating South Korea's efficient public transit system, crucial for newcomers like English teachers and expats. Key topics include:\n\n1. **Starter Essentials**: Purchase a T-money card for seamless travel across subways, buses, and taxis; download Naver Map for navigation; and Kakao T for ride-hailing.\n2. **T-money Card**: Available in convenience stores with a minimal setup fee, allowing convenient topping up and usage across various transport modes. It features a 30-minute transfer discount, enhancing cost efficiency.\n3. **Seoul Subway**: Detailed overview with English signage and safety measures. Rush hours are crowded, but frequent trains mitigate wait times.\n4. **Navigation Apps**: Naver Map favors detailed transit navigation, while Kakao Map integrates taxi booking for easier mobility.\n5. **Bus System and Taxis**: Differentiates between city and express buses, and details on ride-hailing apps like Kakao T and newcomers like k.ride.\n6. **KTX**: High-speed rail options, booking tips, and KORAIL Pass benefits for frequent travelers.\n7. **Airport Transfers**: Discusses various transportation methods from Incheon Airport, recommending the AREX express train and limousine buses.\n8. **Accessibility**: Information on services for people with disabilities and English-language assistance.\n9. **Budgeting**: Cost breakdown for monthly transit expenses for teachers.\n10. **Pro Tips**: Suggestions to optimize commuting habits and avoid common pitfalls.\n\nBy mastering these elements within the first week, expats can efficiently navigate and budget their transit needs throughout their stay."

        "markdown": "![Thumbnail (1920x1080)](https://i.ytimg.com/vi/ljpPsMpopH4/maxresdefault.jpg)\n# [Korea Is Now Solving The NO.1 Tourist Complaint! (+When It Is Scheduled)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ljpPsMpopH4)\n\n**Visibility**: Public\n**Uploaded by**: [GAMJA IN SEOUL](https://www.youtube.com/@GAMJAINSEOUL)\n**Uploaded at**: 2026-10-11\n**Published at**: \n**Length**: 08:25\n**Views**: 21944\n**Likes**: 198\n**Category**: Travel & Events\n\n## Description\n\n```\nAre they really discontinuing the Climate Card? 😱 Can you finally use a foreign credit card to reload your T-Money card? 💳✨ There is a massive misunderstanding going around about Korea's public transit updates, and today I am clearing it all up for you! 💡🇰🇷 \n\nWe also cover the groundbreaking \"Open-Loop\" payment update coming in 2027 that will officially let you tap your home Visa or Mastercard directly on Seoul buses and subways! 🚀🚌 \n\n👇 Skip the planning stress and let me guide you! 🗺️✨\nBook a custom, private tour of Korea with me (Licensed Tour Guide): 🙋‍♂️🇰🇷\n🔗 https://forms.gle/ghgS7snQgEeZnTLy6\n\n📰 Transit News Source:\nhttps://www.koreaherald.com/article/10878189\n\n⏱️ Video Chapters:\n0:00 - The massive confusion about Korea transit updates 🤯\n0:44 - The 3 current transit options for tourists 🎫\n1:04 - T-Money Card explained (Pros & Cons) 🪪\n2:04 - Climate Card explained (Seoul Unlimited Pass) ♾️\n2:40 - Single Ride Tickets 🎟️\n3:16 - Rumor 1: Can you use foreign credit cards at transit machines now? 💳\n4:16 - Rumor 2: Is the Climate Card getting discontinued? ❌\n4:57 - HUGE NEWS: Open-Loop Credit Card payments coming in 2027 🚀\n7:01 - Build your dream Korea itinerary with me! 📝✨\n\n#koreatravel #tmoney #seoultravel #KoreaTransit #ClimateCard\n```\n\n## Transcript\n\nRecently, I’ve been getting comments like this...\n“Can you use a credit card to reload your transit card in Korea now? I saw the news and other people on YouTube saying it!”\n“I saw people on YouTube saying the Climate Card is discontinuing. Am I stuck with T-Money now?”\nThere seems to be a huge misunderstanding going around about these updates.\nToday, I’m going to make these two questions crystal clear for you.\nYou’ll also learn about a massive, ongoing update that will change how tourists travel in Korea in the near future.\nBy the way, this is Gamja!\nBefore I break down what was actually updated and why people are confused, let’s quickly cover the basics.\nIf you’re a tourist coming to Korea, you currently have three main options for public transportation:\n1. T-Money (The Prepaid Card System)\n2. The Climate Card (The Unlimited Pass System)\n3. Single Ride Tickets\nLet’s unpack these one by one so you know exactly what they do and what critical limitations they have.\nOption 1: T-Money\nT-Money is the most common transit card in Korea, operating on a prepaid network.\nYou load funds onto the card, and every time you tap into a subway station or hop on a bus, the fare is automatically deducted.\nIt’s super straightforward and works nationwide.\nSeoul, Busan, Gyeongju, Jeju Island, everywhere.\nIn fact, fancy travel cards like WOWPASS and NAMANE\nuse the T-Money network for their transit features too.\nThe physical card costs around 4,000 KRW (about $3 USD),\nand the balance virtually never expires.\nIf you leave money on it and come back to Korea a decade later,\nthat balance will still be waiting for you.\nThe problem, though, is how you pay for it.\nUp until recently, and still for the most part today,\nyou MUST use physical Korean Won cash to reload funds onto a T-Money card at vending machines or convenience stores.\nI know, it’s a bit old school, but that's just how the system was built.\nWithout a doubt, this has been the number one tourist complaint for years.\nOption 2: Climate Card\nThe Climate Card is Seoul’s unlimited day-pass system.\nYou can purchase passes for 1, 2, 3, 5, 7, or 30 days.\nDuring those active days, you get unlimited rides on Seoul’s subways and city buses for a single flat fee.\nUnlike T-Money, you can buy and charge this card using international credit cards from home.\nSounds like it just solved the T-Money problem and only got better, right?\nExcept there’s a major geographic limitation.\nIt is a Seoul-only card.\nThat means it will not work on subways or buses in Busan, Gyeongju, or even at Incheon Airport.\nOption 3: Single Ride Tickets\nThis is a single-use subway ticket that you purchase directly at station vending machines.\nYou select your destination, pay your fare plus a small refundable deposit,\nride the train, and return the card at your arrival station to get your deposit back.\nBut this one is subway-only. You cannot use it on buses,\nand you miss out on the free transfer system between subways and buses.\nNow that you know the three options, let's address the big question:\nWhat on earth is the recent news that confused everyone on YouTube and TikTok?\nLet's tackle the two biggest rumors.\nRumor #1: \"You can now reload your transit card with a foreign credit card!\"\nYES and NO.\nHere is what actually happened.\nIn March 2026, Seoul installed 440 brand-new transportation card vending machines across 273 subway stations.\nThese new machines officially allow international tourists to pay using foreign credit cards.\nVisa, Mastercard, JCB, UnionPay, and mobile payments.\nBUT HERE IS THE DISTINCTION!\nYou can use your foreign credit card at these machines to buy or reload the Climate Card, and to buy Single-Journey Tickets.\nYou CANNOT use foreign credit cards to reload a standard T-Money card at these machines.\nSo they basically solved all the other problems except for the biggest one.\nHowever, they did manage to let you use iPhone to tap on and off the public transportation.\nAND reload T-Money balance online using your credit card.\nYou will need the T-Money Mobile App installed in your iPhone.\nIf you decide to use your iPhone, no need for a physical T-Money Card anymore.\nAndroid phones are currently not supported.\nRumor #2: \"The Climate Card is being discontinued!\"\nYES AND NO again.\nCurrently, the Climate Card offers 1, 2, 3, 5, 7, and 30-day passes.\nIt’s only the 30-day pass that’s being discontinued.\nThat 30-day pass is actually being merged into something else that locals might be interested in not the tourists.\nThe other short-term passes are still there and fully functioning just like before.\nSo, unless you were planning to get the 30-day pass, you have absolutely nothing to worry about.\nSo that was what’s been happening recently\nand now this brings us to the latest, most exciting piece of news.\nT-Money officially signed a partnership agreement with Visa, Mastercard, UnionPay, JCB, and Hana Card.\nTheir objective is to officially build an Open-Loop Payment System in South Korea.\nLet’s break down what \"Open-Loop\" actually means in plain English.\nOpen-Loop means no transit card required. No T-Money card. No Climate Card. No stopping at subway vending machines. No hunting for cash ATMs.\nYou simply walk onto a bus or through a subway gate, take out the Visa, Mastercard, or mobile wallet you brought from home, tap it directly on the reader, and walk through.\nHere is the exact timeline for how this Open-Loop system is rolling out:\nLate 2026 (Right Now): T-Money and international card networks are finalizing system development, and security testing.\n2027 (Phase 1 Launch): Open-loop payment will be officially available on Seoul city buses!\nStarting in 2027, you will be able to tap your personal foreign credit card directly onto Seoul buses.\nFollowing Seoul city buses, the system will expand to Incheon buses and Airport Limousine buses.\n2027 to 2030 (Full Metropolitan Expansion): Seoul plans to progressively upgrade hardware across all subway turnstiles, buses, and regional transfer lines,\nwith a long-term goal of complete open-loop integration across the entire metropolitan region by 2030.\nThis is huge!\nIt means the days of struggling to understand Korean transit cards are officially done.\nBut of course, talk is cheap...\nMassive infrastructure updates like this can easily face setbacks.\nJust know the update is in progress, but keep in mind that the timeline might get delayed from their original plan.\nTravel should be about making memories, tasting amazing food, and exploring new cultures.\nNot stressing over how to tap through a subway turnstile.\nKorea's transit system was already one of the cleanest, safest, and most efficient in the world.\nNow, by breaking down these payment barriers,\nit’s finally becoming one of the most accessible for travelers worldwide.\nIf this video helped clear up the confusion for you,\nhit that like button, subscribe to Gamja In Seoul,\nand drop any questions you have in the comments below.\nI read and answer as many as I can!\nAnd before you go, If you wish to make your travel to the next level and skip all this planning stress entirely,\nyou can be my next guest for a tour in Korea!\nI am a licensed tour guide and I am also a registered travel agency owner.\nWe can build your itinerary from scratch together and we go on to a tour just like that!\nIf you're interested, please fill in this Google Form and I’ll personally reply to you on the email.\nThis gives me the basic information I need to know to start with your itinerary.\nAnd the price as of now is this right here on the screen.\nPlease note that this is not your final price chart.\nAs we discuss on your tailored itinerary,\nthe price may change accordingly.\nBut of course, it won’t change too much, just letting you know!\nIn the price you pay, I am actually covering your entrance ticket to sites and a lunch.\nPrice may change in the future, but for now, this is the price.\nSince it’s a fully customizable private tour,\njust let me know what you need, what you want or what you don’t want.\nFor example, it doesn’t need to be Seoul only and we can even go onto a road trip just like you are visiting a friend in Korea!\nI once had customers who wanted to see the “non-touristy” spots away from Seoul,\nso I went on a road trip to the East Coast in my private car.\nI am super excited to host you in Korea.\nReserve your spot before it’s all fully booked.\nCurrently, I am half fully booked for the few upcoming months.\nUntil next time, safe travels, and I’ll see you in the next video!",

        "summary": "The video discusses updates on public transit options for tourists in Korea, addressing common misconceptions about using foreign credit cards and the Climate Card. It clarifies that the T-Money transit card can currently only be reloaded with Korean cash, while the Climate Card allows for international credit card usage but is limited to Seoul operations. The video introduces an upcoming 'Open-Loop' payment system, set to launch in phases from 2027, which will allow travelers to use their foreign credit cards directly on public transit. Key highlights include: 1. T-Money operates nationwide but can only be reloaded with cash. 2. The Climate Card offers unlimited rides in Seoul and can be reloaded with international credit cards, except for the 30-day pass which is being discontinued. 3. 'Open-Loop' will eliminate the need for transit cards, allowing users to tap their credit cards directly beginning in late 2026. This update aims to improve accessibility and reduce the confusion for tourists navigating public transit in Korea."

      {
        "url": "https://english.visitkorea.or.kr/svc/contents/contentsView.do?vcontsId=140663",
        "title": "Transportation Cards- VISITKOREA",
        "description": "You can purchase and load these cards at convenience stores nationwide.\n\nThe card can be loaded at ticket vending machines inside subway stations with the one-day pass starting at 5,000 won; two-day at 8,000 won; 3-day at 10,000 won; 5-day at 15,000 won; and 7-day at 20,000 won.\n\n### < FAQ >\nThe card costs 3,000-5,000 won and can be recharged at subway ticket vending machines (up to 500,000 won).\n\nYes. Options include: 1-day (15,000 won), 2-day (23,000 won), 3-day (30,500 won), 5-day (47,500 won), and 7-day (64,500 won).\n\nThe physical card costs 3,000 won and can be loaded at subway station kiosks. Pass options: 1-day (5,000 won), 2-day (8,000 won), 3-day (10,000 won), 5-day (15,000 won), 7-day (20,000 won).\n\nYes. Both cards also offer free transfer discounts when switching between buses and subways within a set time window.",
        "position": 5,
        "markdown": "**Contents View**\n\n- [HOME](https://english.visitkorea.or.kr/svc/main/index.do)\n- Contents View\n\n- - # Transportation Cards\n\n\n\n\n\n    - 03/31/2023\n\n    - 438.4K\n\n      My Likes\n\n      9\n\n      common.share\n\n      My Bookmarks\n\n      17\n\n\n![copy-white sharing button](https://platform-cdn.sharethis.com/img/copy-white.svg)\n\n![facebook-white sharing button](https://platform-cdn.sharethis.com/img/facebook-white.svg)\n\n![twitter-white sharing button](https://platform-cdn.sharethis.com/img/twitter-white.svg)\n\n  - - Bus\\_Tour\n\n    - Transportation\\_card\n\n    - public\\_transport\n\n    - Intercity\\_Bus\\_Terminal\n\n    - subway\n\n    - city\\_tour\\_bus\n\n    - express\\_bus\n- ## Transportation Cards\n\nIt can be quite a hassle to purchase a ticket or pay with cash each and every time you use public transportation during your trip. For convenience, try using a Korean transportation card (Tmoney Card, EZL Card, WOWPASS, Climate Card)! These are reloadable prepaid cards that do not require an account. Not only do they offer transfer discount benefits on public transportation, but most cards can be used for making payments at both online and offline stores, making them extremely convenient. Make your trip in Korea more easy and convenient with a simple card.\n\n**Tmoney Card & EZL Card**\n\nYou can purchase and load these cards at convenience stores nationwide. They can be used for all public transportation and at affiliated stores that display the logos of these cards. If you are under the age of 18 and wish to receive discount benefits for public transportation fees, you must register your date of birth when purchasing a card.\n\nThe Tmoney Card has the largest number of affiliated stores in Korea. There is also a foreigner-exclusive Tmoney card called Mpass Card that offers tourist-friendly and money-saving benefits, so travelers are recommended to compare the benefits before selecting a card.\n\n\\+ Visit the following websites for more information\n\nTmoney website: [eng.tmoney.co.kr](https://eng.tmoney.co.kr/en/aeb/main/main/readMain.dev \"go to web\") (English)\n\nTmoney guide: [pay.tmoney.co.kr](https://pay.tmoney.co.kr/ncs/pct/ugd/ReadFrgnGd.dev \"go to web\") (English, Japanese, Chinese)\n\nEZL Card website: [myezl.com](https://myezl.com/ezl/sub/foreigner_en.do \"go to web\")(English, Japanese, Chinese)\n\n**Foreigner-exclusive Tmoney Card, Mpass**\n\n\nA time-limited transportation card that offers rides on the subway and city buses in Seoul and AREX (excluding express trains) for up to 20 times a day. Card types include 1-day pass (15,000 won), 2-day pass (23,000 won), 3-day pass (30,500 won), 5-day pass (47,500 won), and 7-day pass (64,500 won), and can only be purchased in Korean won cash. The card is only valid for 30 days starting from the issued date and if you purchase it after 17:00, you can automatically receive a 3,000 won discount. Mpass Card also has the same transportation card features of a Tmoney Card. Reload the card at locations that sell Tmoney Cards and use it to pay for taxis, express buses, intercity buses, or trains.\n\n\\\\* Card issuance and return location: Tmoney Town near Seoul Station, Exit 10 (Tmoney, 1F on Seoul City Tower, 110 Huam-ro, Jung-gu, Seoul / 서울특별시 중구 후암로110 서울시티타워 10층 티머니)\n\n\\\\* Mpass guide: [pay.tmoney.co.kr](https://pay.tmoney.co.kr/ncs/pct/tmnyintd/ReadFrgnMpassEngIntd.dev \"go to web\") (English)\n\n**WOWPASS**\n\n![](https://tong.visitkorea.or.kr/cms/resource_etc/94/3113394_image2_1.png)\n\nWOWPASS is a foreigner-exclusive all-in-one prepaid transportation card which you can use it to pay and exchange currencies. You can use the card like a service-fee free debit card at affiliated stores that accept card payment. To purchase a WOWPASS card, you can use their online website or app and pick up the physical card later at a designated location within the airport or through one of the kiosks installed at 300 different locations, including major subway stations, hotels, and international airports; you can also purchase the card on the spot through the kiosk. In order to buy a WOWPASS card, you will need your passport. Once you get your card, you can use the WOWPASS kiosk to load the card with KRW as well as USD, JPY, CNY, and other accepted foreign currencies. Make sure to register your card on the WOWPASS mobile app to see payment history, as well as your remaining balance, and benefit from cash back and coupon rewards.\n\n\\+ More on WOWPASS: [www.wowpass.io](https://www.wowpass.io/?lang=en_US \"go to web\") (English, Japanese, Chinese, Vietnamese, Thai)\n\n**Climate Card**\n\n\nA Climate Card Short-Term Pass is perfect for those planning to use the public transportation often while traveling in Seoul. This comprehensive transportation card offered by Seoul metropolitan government provides unlimited access to buses and subways in Seoul (excluding Sinbundang Line & GTX) for a select period of one to seven days. A physical Climate Card can be purchased at the Seoul Metro Information Center inside subway stations and select nearby convenience stores for 3,000 won. The card can be loaded at ticket vending machines inside subway stations with the one-day pass starting at 5,000 won; two-day at 8,000 won; 3-day at 10,000 won; 5-day at 15,000 won; and 7-day at 20,000 won. Once loaded, the card becomes activated and cannot be changed or reloaded until its expiration date, so users are advised to purchase and load the card with their itinerary in mind.\n\n\\+ More on Climate Card: [english.seoul.go.kr](https://english.seoul.go.kr/policy/transportation/climate-card/ \"go to web\") (English, Japanese, Chinese)\n\n✔ Compare all transportation cards at a glance\n\n| 7-ELEVEN, etc.)  <br>\\- Information Centers in Seoul subway stations on Lines 1 to 8 <br> \\- Reloadable through subway ticket vending machines inside subway stations (Reload amount limited to 500,000 won max.)  <br>**▶ Price:** 3,000 won-5,000 won (General)<br>**▶ Accepted locations:**<br> \\- Public transportation and affiliated stores displaying the Tmoney Card logo  <br> \\- Expressway toll gates <br> \\- Public phones  <br> \\- F&B vending machines, unmanned photo booths, etc. in subway stations  <br>**▶ Tips:**<br> \\- If you are under the age of 18 and wish to receive discount [www.myezl.com](https://myezl.com/ezl/sub/foreigner_en.do) (English, Japanese, Chinese) |\n| **WOWPASS**<br>![](https://tong.visitkorea.or.kr/cms/resource_etc/95/3113395_image2_1.png) | **▶Retail locations:**<br> \\- WOWPASS app: Search “WOWPASS” on Google Play or App Store<br> \\- WOWPASS kiosk: Visit the website or download app to check kiosk locations<br>**▶ Price:** 5,000 won<br>**▶ \\- Myeongdong Tourist information Center <br> \\- Seoul Metro Information Center Line 1-8 (cash only) and the new transportation card vending machines<br> \\- Convenience stores (GS25, CU, 7-11, Emart24) near subway stations (Line 9, Ui-Sinseol Line, and Sillim Line)<br>**▶ Price:**<br> \\- Physical card 3,000 won <br> \\- Short-Term Passes: 1-day 5,000 won / 2-day 8,000 won / 3-day 10,000 won / 5-day 15,000 won / 7-day 20,000 won<br> \\\\* 30-day Climate Card (prepaid & postpaid) discontinued effective October 1, 2026<br>**▶ Accepted locations:**<br> \\- Seoul-based subway lines, receive a refund; it is recommended travelers spend all the money deposited to the card<br> \\- The short-term pass is activated from the moment the card is loaded<br> \\- Getting off at a station outside of the service range will result in additional fares<br>**▶ Advantages:**<br> \\- Unlimited use of public transportation with one-time load<br> \\- Discount on various cultural facilities in Seoul  <br>**▶ Website:** [www.wowpass](https://english.seoul.go.kr/policy/transportation/climate-card/) (English, Japanese, Chinese) |\n\n### < FAQ >\n\nQ1. What transportation cards can tourists use in Korea? A. Tourists in Korea can use four main transportation cards: Tmoney Card, EZL Card, WOWPASS, and Climate Card. Tmoney and EZL are chargeable prepaid cards available at convenience stores nationwide that work on all public transportation and offer transfer discounts. WOWPASS is a foreigner-exclusive all-in-one card that also supports currency exchange. Climate Card is Seoul's unlimited public transit pass available in 1- to 7-day options.\n\nQ2. Where can I buy a Tmoney Card in Korea?A. Tmoney Cards are sold at convenience stores nationwide (GS25, CU, 7-ELEVEN, etc.) and at Seoul subway station information centers (Lines 1-8). The card costs 3,000-5,000 won and can be recharged at subway ticket vending machines (up to 500,000 won). Travelers under 18 must register their date of birth at a convenience store to receive discount benefits on public transportation fares.\n\nQ3. Is there a tourist transportation pass for Korea? A. Yes. The Mpass Card is a foreigner-exclusive Tmoney card offering up to 20 rides per day on Seoul subways, city buses, and AREX (excluding express trains) for a fixed period. Options include: 1-day (15,000 won), 2-day (23,000 won), 3-day (30,500 won), 5-day (47,500 won), and 7-day (64,500 won). Purchase with Korean won cash only. The card is valid for 30 days from the issue date. Cards are issued and returned at Tmoney Town near Seoul Station Exit 10.\n\nQ4. What is WOWPASS and how do I get one in Korea? A. WOWPASS is a foreigner-exclusive prepaid card combining transportation, shopping payments, and currency exchange. You can purchase it via the WOWPASS app or at kiosks located at major airports, subway stations, and hotels (430+ locations). A passport is required to buy the card (5,000 won). Once purchased, you can load Korean won or foreign currencies (USD, JPY, CNY, etc.) at any WOWPASS kiosk. Register the card on the WOWPASS app to track balance, payment history, and access cashback and coupon benefits.\n\nQ5. What is the Climate Card and how much does it cost?A. The Climate Card is Seoul's public transportation pass offering unrestricted access to buses and subways (Seoul lines + Gimpo Gold Line). The physical card costs 3,000 won and can be loaded at subway station kiosks. Pass options: 1-day (5,000 won), 2-day (8,000 won), 3-day (10,000 won), 5-day (15,000 won), 7-day (20,000 won).\n\nQ6. Can I use a Korean transportation card on buses, subways, and taxis?A. Yes. Tmoney and EZL cards work on virtually all public transportation in Korea including subways, city buses, express buses, intercity buses, taxis, and trains. Both cards also offer free transfer discounts when switching between buses and subways within a set time window. Cards can also be used at affiliated convenience stores, cafes, vending machines, and other retail locations displaying the card logos.\n\nQ7. Can I get a refund on my Korean transportation card balance before leaving?A. For Tmoney Card, refunds of over 50,000 won must be processed at Tmoney Town near Seoul Station Exit 10. Smaller amounts can be refunded at convenience stores. For EZL Card, only refunds under 20,000 won are available at convenience stores. WOWPASS remaining balance can be withdrawn as Korean won cash at any WOWPASS kiosk. For Climate Card, foreigners without a Korean bank account can visit Tmoney headquarters for a refund — it is recommended to spend the balance before departure.\n\nThis page was last updated in August 2026, and therefore information may differ from what is presented here.\n\n1330 Korea Travel Helpline:+82-2-1330 (Korean, English, Japanese, Chinese, Russian, Vietnamese, Thai, and Indonesian)\n\nClose\n\n**This website uses cookies.**\n\nThis website uses cookies to enhance your experience.\n\nBy selecting “Allow all cookies,” you are giving your consent for us to set your cookies.\n\nFor more information on cookies, refer to the Cookie Policy.\n\n\nAllow all cookiesCookie settings\n\nClose\n\n**Purpose of collection and use of cookies**\n\nThis website uses cookies to enhance your experience.\n\nAllow all cookiesBlock all cookies\n\n- Necessary cookies\n\n\n\n\n\n\nNecessary cookies are required for features such as website search and security.         "summary": "The page provides information on various transportation cards available for tourists in South Korea, emphasizing their convenience and benefits. The main cards outlined include: \n1. **Tmoney Card**: Widely accepted for public transportation and affiliated stores; available at convenience stores with a reloadable feature. Discount benefits for users under 18 require registration.\n2. **EZL Card**: Similar to Tmoney but offers card tap features and various designs; available at convenience stores.\n3. **WOWPASS**: An all-in-one prepaid card for transportation and currency exchange, available for purchase online and at airport kiosks, requiring a passport for registration.\n4. **Climate Card**: Provides unlimited access to Seoul's public transport systems for up to 7 days, available for purchase at convenient locations.\n\nEach card has specific pricing, features, and purchase locations. Additionally, a FAQ section addresses common inquiries regarding card usage, refunds, and benefits."

        "description": "## Why a transit card is not optional in Busan\nNo Visa contactless.\n\n|  |  |\n|-|-|\n| **Cost** | ₩2,500 card deposit + initial load (₩10,000–20,000 typical) |\n| **Fare saving** | ₩1,600 card vs ₩1,700 cash per journey |\n| **Transfer window** | 30 minutes between metro/bus for discount |\n\n## T-money vs Cashbee: which should you buy?\nBoth cost the same (₩2,500 card deposit plus your initial load), and both do the same job in Busan.\n\n## Where T-money works in Busan\n### City buses\nBus fare payment in Busan is handled almost entirely by card tap — the alternative is having exact change in coins and notes, since bus drivers cannot make change.\n\nTap on when you board, tap again when you exit — both taps matter for the transfer discount system explained below. If you forget to tap off, you will not be charged extra, but the system will not register a completed journey, which affects transfer eligibility.\n\n### Some taxis\nPayment by T-money is tap-on-the-reader at the end of the journey.\n\nThe meter fare is the full fare.\n\n## Connecting T-money to the wider Busan experience\nIf you would rather see the coastal highlights in one go than piece together buses and metro transfers, this guided tour with the Blueline Park Beach Train is one way to do it.\n\n[GetYourGuideBusan Top Highlights with Blueline Park Beach TrainFull day · Beach trainCheck availability →](https://www.getyourguide.com/busan-l32281/busan-city-s-top-highlights-with-blueline-park-beach-train-t602067/?partner_id=LDXYA0P&cmp=busan-trip&utm_medium=online_publisher)\n\nIf you are thinking about cost more broadly, the [Busan on a budget guide](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/busan-on-a-budget/) includes transit costs in the overall daily spend estimates. For visitors wondering whether the [Busan City Pass](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/busan-city-pass/) is worth it alongside a transit card — those are two different products addressing different needs, and the City Pass guide explains when the combination makes sense.\n\nOn taxis, buses, in convenience stores, at restaurants — the price you see is the price you pay.         "markdown": "[Skip to content](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/tmoney-cashbee-cards/#main-content)\n\n![T-money and Cashbee cards: the essential guide for getting around Busan](https://www.busan-trip.com/images/guides/tmoney-cashbee-cards.jpg)\n\nQuick Answer\n\n**Do I need a T-money card for Busan's metro?**\n\nYes, practically speaking. Busan's metro does not accept contactless credit cards or Apple Pay as of 2026 — you pay with a T-money card or buy a single-journey cash token each time. T-money also gives you a 10% discount on every ride and free transfers between metro and bus within 30 minutes. Buy one at any convenience store before your first journey.\n\n## Why a transit card is not optional in Busan\n\nBusan has a clean, well-signed, air-conditioned metro system with four lines covering most places visitors want to reach — Haeundae, Gwangalli, Seomyeon, Nampo, the KTX station at Busan Station, Gimhae Airport on the dedicated airport rail. On paper, it sounds straightforward. In practice, there is one catch that catches a surprising number of first-time visitors off guard: **Busan’s metro does not accept tap-to-pay credit cards**.\n\nNo Visa contactless. No Mastercard tap. No Apple Pay. No Google Pay. As of mid-2026, the fare gates in Busan accept two things: a T-money transit card, or a single-journey token purchased from a ticket machine with cash. That is it.\n\nThis matters because travellers who arrive with a modern wallet — expecting to tap their phone or credit card like they would in London, Singapore, or Tokyo — walk up to the fare gate and find themselves with no way through. The alternative (single-journey tokens) works, but it means queuing at a ticket machine for every single journey, paying the slightly higher cash fare of ₩1,700 instead of the card fare of ₩1,600, and losing all transfer discounts. Over a week of daily metro use, the friction and the cost difference add up.\n\nThe fix is simple: buy a T-money card before or immediately upon arrival. This guide tells you exactly how.\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| **Where to buy** | Gimhae Airport arrivals, or any GS25/CU/7-Eleven |\n| **Cost** | ₩2,500 card deposit + initial load (₩10,000–20,000 typical) |\n| **Fare saving** | ₩1,600 card vs ₩1,700 cash per journey |\n| **Transfer window** | 30 minutes between metro/bus for discount |\n| **Refund** | Balance + ₩2,500 deposit minus ₩500 fee, at any major convenience store |\n\nFor the full picture of how Busan’s metro system works — lines, zones, journey times — see the [Busan metro guide](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/busan-metro-guide/). If you are arriving directly from the airport and want to plan your first journey into the city, the [Gimhae Airport to city centre guide](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/gimhae-airport-to-city/) covers the airport rail and bus options in detail.\n\n* * *\n\n## T-money and Cashbee: what they are\n\n**T-money** is South Korea’s national transit card. It was introduced in Seoul and expanded nationwide; today it is accepted on most public transport across the country — metro systems, intercity and city buses, some taxis — and also at convenience stores for everyday purchases. Think of it as an RFID chip on a card that you preload with Korean won, then tap to pay. The balance sits on the chip, not on an account, which means it works without internet, phone signal, or a bank card.\n\n**Cashbee** is a competing card that originated in Busan and the southeastern region of Korea. Historically it was the dominant transit card in Busan while T-money dominated Seoul. Today that distinction has largely dissolved: both cards are accepted on Busan metro, city buses, and most convenience stores in the region. You are unlikely to find a situation in Busan where one works and the other does not.\n\nNeither card is linked to your identity or bank account. You load cash onto them and spend down the balance. If you lose one, the remaining balance is gone — there is no recovery mechanism unless you registered the card (which most visitors do not do).\n\n* * *\n\n## T-money vs Cashbee: which should you buy?\n\nFor the practical purposes of a trip to Busan, the cards are interchangeable on transit. The fare, the transfer discount, and the top-up process are identical. The meaningful difference is geographic scope:\n\n**T-money** is the better choice if you are also travelling to Seoul, Gyeongju, Daegu, or anywhere else in Korea during your trip. It is accepted on Seoul’s metro, on many intercity buses, and widely recognised nationwide. If you ride the KTX back to Seoul, you can use the same T-money card on Seoul’s subway from the moment you arrive.\n\n**Cashbee** is equally fine if you are staying in Busan for your entire visit. It works on everything that matters locally. It is sold at Family Mart and other convenience stores in Busan; in Seoul-based or national chains like GS25, T-money tends to be more prominently stocked.\n\n**Verdict for most visitors**: buy T-money. Not because Cashbee is worse, but because T-money’s national coverage removes one decision if your itinerary changes. Both cost the same (₩2,500 card deposit plus your initial load), and both do the same job in Busan.\n\n* * *\n\n## Where to buy a card\n\n### At Gimhae Airport\n\nThe fastest option: convenience stores in the arrivals hall sell both T-money and Cashbee. Before you clear the arrivals area, grab a card, load ₩10,000–20,000, and you are ready to board the airport rail or bus immediately. There is no reason to hunt for a card after you land — it is right there.\n\nThe [Gimhae Airport to city centre guide](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/gimhae-airport-to-city/) covers the journey options in more detail.\n\n### At metro stations\n\nMost metro stations have vending machines that sell single-journey tokens. Some machines also sell T-money cards, but this varies by station. Do not rely on this as your primary plan — convenience stores are more reliable for purchasing a new card.\n\n### At convenience stores — the easiest option\n\nGS25, CU, 7-Eleven, Family Mart, and Emart24 all sell T-money cards. There is at least one of these within a short walk of almost every metro station in Busan. This is where the vast majority of locals buy and top up their cards.\n\nOne thing to note: the cards are usually **behind the counter**, not on the shelves. Walk in, go to the cashier, and ask for a “T-money card” — they will know immediately what you mean. The staff at most Busan convenience stores are accustomed to foreign visitors asking for transit cards and will show you the options without needing Korean.\n\n**Cost**: ₩2,500 deposit for the card itself, plus however much you want to load initially. The ₩2,500 deposit is refunded when you return the card, minus a ₩500 handling fee (see the refund section below).\n\n* * *\n\n## How to load (top up) your card\n\n### At metro station machines\n\nEvery metro station has fare machines, and all of them have an English-language option. Select “T-money recharge” or equivalent on the screen, tap your card on the reader, insert cash, and confirm. The minimum top-up amount is ₩1,000, but machines typically prompt for round amounts — ₩5,000, ₩10,000, ₩20,000. Loading ₩10,000 before a day of exploring gives you comfortable headroom; a standard metro journey costs ₩1,600, so that covers six or seven rides with change left over.\n\n### At convenience store counters\n\nHand your card and the cash you want to load to the cashier and say “T-money charge” (or “tee-moni chaji” if you want to attempt Korean phonetics). They will tap the card on their reader, process the amount, and hand it back. Straightforward, fast, no language barrier issues in practice.\n\nKeep the receipt if you want a record of the transaction — the machines and store systems do not send confirmation messages.\n\n### Recommended load amounts\n\nFor a week in Busan with daily metro use and some bus journeys: ₩20,000–30,000 total across your stay is typically sufficient. Load in increments that feel comfortable — ₩10,000 at a time is a sensible unit since metro machines and convenience stores both handle it cleanly. If you are heading to areas further from the centre (Dadaepo Beach on the western end of Line 1, for instance), top up before you go rather than hunting for machines at quieter stations.\n\n* * *\n\n## Where T-money works in Busan\n\n### Busan Metro — all four lines\n\nT-money and Cashbee work on every line of the Busan metro: Line 1 (orange, north-south from Nopo to Dadaepo Beach), Line 2 (green, east-west through Haeundae and Seomyeon), Line 3 (brown, inner circular), and Line 4 (blue, northeastern route via Minam). The fare is ₩1,600 per journey at the base zone, versus ₩1,700 if you pay cash — a 6% saving on each individual ride, which compounds over a trip with multiple daily journeys.\n\nFor route planning on all four lines, the [Busan metro guide](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/busan-metro-guide/) has station-by-station details.\n\n### City buses\n\nT-money is even more important on buses than it is on the metro. Bus fare payment in Busan is handled almost entirely by card tap — the alternative is having exact change in coins and notes, since bus drivers cannot make change. Getting onto a Busan city bus with only a credit card and no T-money means either you have exact change ready (unlikely for tourists) or you negotiate with an impatient queue behind you.\n\nTap on when you board, tap again when you exit — both taps matter for the transfer discount system explained below. If you forget to tap off, you will not be charged extra, but the system will not register a completed journey, which affects transfer eligibility.\n\n### Some taxis\n\nMany Busan taxis accept T-money. Look for a T-money sticker on the window or dashboard — it is usually visible from outside the vehicle. Payment by T-money is tap-on-the-reader at the end of the journey. Not all taxis participate, and there is no penalty for asking the driver before you get in. Kakao Taxi (Korea’s ride-hailing app) shows payment method options when booking.\n\nNo tipping in Korea — in taxis or anywhere else. The meter fare is the full fare.\n\n### Convenience stores\n\nEvery GS25, CU, 7-Eleven, Family Mart, and Emart24 accepts T-money for purchases. This makes the card genuinely useful beyond transit: you can pick up water, snacks, a coffee from the self-serve machine, or triangle kimbap and pay by tapping the card on the reader at the till. The balance is deducted from your card just as it would be for a metro fare.\n\nThis also gives you a natural way to use down remaining balance at the end of your trip — more on that below.\n\n### What T-money does not cover\n\nT-money is not a general-purpose payment card in Korea. It will not work at most restaurants, cafes, shops, museums, or tourist attractions. Most of those accept credit cards (Visa and Mastercard are widely accepted), and many now accept Apple Pay and Samsung Pay at the point-of-sale terminal. The divide is specifically transit and convenience stores versus everything else.\n\n* * *\n\n## The transfer discount: how it works and why it matters\n\nThis is the feature most visitors either never learn about or understand correctly.\n\nWhen you exit a metro station or get off a bus, a 30-minute window opens. If you board another bus or metro within that 30 minutes, the second journey is either free (if the fare has already been “used”) or significantly discounted. The system is designed to make multi-leg journeys — a metro to a bus, or a bus to a metro — feel like a single continuous trip rather than two separate fares.\n\n**In practice**: tap off when you leave the metro or bus (do not skip this step). Within 30 minutes, tap on to board your next bus or metro. Your card will be charged a reduced amount or nothing, depending on whether the combined distance exceeds the base fare zone. The machine does not ask you to confirm anything — it just calculates and deducts correctly.\n\nThis discount is only available with T-money or Cashbee. Single-journey tokens bought with cash get no transfer benefit.\n\nFor visitors doing combinations like metro to Haeundae followed by a bus along the beach strip, or metro to Nampo-dong followed by a local bus to the fishing port, the savings and convenience are meaningful over a full day.\n\n* * *\n\n## Checking your balance\n\nTwo easy options:\n\n**At metro entrance gates**: most stations have a card reader beside the fare gates specifically for balance checks. Tap your card and the remaining balance displays. Takes about two seconds.\n\n**At convenience store counters**: hand the card to the cashier or tap it on the reader at the till and ask for the balance. They will show you the screen or tell you the amount.\n\nThere is no app required and no account registration needed. The balance is stored on the card chip itself.\n\n* * *\n\n## What to do with the card at the end of your trip\n\nYou have three sensible options:\n\n**Keep it for your next visit.** T-money and Cashbee cards do not expire. If you plan to return to Korea, leaving ₩3,000–5,000 on the card and keeping it in your travel wallet is a completely valid choice. You land, the card still works, and you top up at the airport convenience store.\n\n**Use up the remaining balance at convenience stores.** In the last day or two of your trip, stop topping up and instead spend down the balance on snacks, drinks, and sundries at GS25, CU, or 7-Eleven. If you time it right you arrive at the airport with near-zero balance, no refund hassle.\n\n**Refund at a convenience store.** Take the card to any GS25, CU, 7-Eleven, Family Mart, or Emart24 and ask for a T-money refund. They will process it at the till. You receive the remaining balance **plus the ₩2,500 card deposit, minus a ₩500 handling fee**. So if you have ₩3,000 left on the card, you get back ₩5,000 total (₩3,000 balance + ₩2,500 deposit - ₩500 fee). The minimum refundable balance after the fee is applied is ₩50 — if your card has less than that remaining after deducting the fee, some stores may decline the refund or apply their own minimum.\n\nNote that the refund covers the deposit only at stores that are equipped to handle it — all the major chains are, but a very small independent convenience store occasionally is not. Stick to GS25, CU, or 7-Eleven and you will not have problems.\n\n* * *\n\n## Credit cards, Apple Pay, and the cash alternative\n\nIt is worth being clear on what does and does not work on Busan transit, because misinformation circulates online and gets people into trouble.\n\n**Contactless credit/debit cards (Visa, Mastercard tap)**: do not work on Busan metro fare gates or city buses as of 2026. This includes physical cards with NFC chips and cards added to phone wallets.\n\n**Apple Pay and Google Pay**: work at many retail shops and some restaurants in Korea, but not on Busan transit. The metro system runs on a proprietary RFID network that requires T-money or Cashbee. This is a infrastructure decision, not a feature gap that gets patched by software updates.\n\n**Single-journey tokens**: bought at metro vending machines, work for one journey, no transfers. The machine takes cash (Korean won only). You pay ₩1,700 per token, queue each time, and get no transfer discount. A perfectly functional backup if your card is lost or depleted and you need one journey urgently — not a viable daily strategy.\n\n**Cash on buses**: technically possible if you have exact change, but impractical for tourists. The driver cannot make change. Carrying the precise fare for each bus trip in coins is an avoidable headache.\n\nThe bottom line: T-money resolves all of this. The cards are cheap, available everywhere, and work on everything transit-related in the city.\n\n* * *\n\n## Practical tips for using your card\n\n**Load enough before heading to less central neighbourhoods.** Metro stations in areas like Dadaepo, Hadan, or the outer reaches of Line 3 and Line 4 have fewer convenience stores nearby. A Seomyeon-area top-up before heading west on Line 1 is easier than scrambling at a quiet suburban station.\n\n**Keep the card accessible.** T-money works with a tap — you do not need to take it out of a wallet if the wallet is thin enough, but most fare gates work best with the card directly on the reader. A card holder on the back of your phone or a dedicated card slot in a travel wallet makes the tapping process faster and avoids the delay of digging through a full bag with a queue forming.\n\n**Card not registered means balance not recoverable if lost.** Unlike some national transit systems that offer online account registration and balance transfer, the standard tourist T-money card balance is tied to the chip. If the card is lost or stolen, the balance on it is gone. This is not a reason to panic — ₩10,000 is about $7 — but it is a reason not to preload more than you need for a few days at a time.\n\n**The airport rail from Gimhae accepts T-money.** The BGLRT (Busan-Gimhae Light Rail Transit) that connects Gimhae Airport to Sasang Station (where you transfer to Line 2) runs on the same T-money system. Buy your card in the arrivals hall before you board.\n\n**Language is not a barrier at top-up machines.** Metro fare machines have English, Chinese, and Japanese menus. For the T-money recharge function specifically, the UI is minimal — tap card, insert cash, confirm amount — even without reading Korean.\n\n* * *\n\n## Connecting T-money to the wider Busan experience\n\nTransit cards are the kind of infrastructure that should be invisible — you buy one, it works, and you stop thinking about it. Getting that first purchase out of the way at the airport convenience store before anything else means you arrive in the city already able to move.\n\nFrom there, the card is your key to the metro network covered in the [Busan metro guide](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/busan-metro-guide/), to the airport connection detailed in the [Gimhae Airport to city guide](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/gimhae-airport-to-city/), and to the broader question of [getting around Busan](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/getting-around-busan/) across its famously sprawling geography.\n\nIf you would rather see the coastal highlights in one go than piece together buses and metro transfers, this guided tour with the Blueline Park Beach Train is one way to do it.\n\n[GetYourGuideBusan Top Highlights with Blueline Park Beach TrainFull day · Beach trainCheck availability →](https://www.getyourguide.com/busan-l32281/busan-city-s-top-highlights-with-blueline-park-beach-train-t602067/?partner_id=LDXYA0P&cmp=busan-trip&utm_medium=online_publisher)\n\nIf you are thinking about cost more broadly, the [Busan on a budget guide](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/busan-on-a-budget/) includes transit costs in the overall daily spend estimates. For visitors wondering whether the [Busan City Pass](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/busan-city-pass/) is worth it alongside a transit card — those are two different products addressing different needs, and the City Pass guide explains when the combination makes sense. And if this is your first time in the city and you want the orienting overview before anything else, the [Busan first-timer guide](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/busan-first-timer-guide/) covers the neighbourhoods, the rhythms, and the priorities before you dive into logistics.\n\nFor currency: if you are still working out how much Korean won to carry and exchange, the [currency converter](https://www.busan-trip.com/tools/currency/) lets you run the numbers in your home currency.\n\nOne last practical note: Korea does not have a tipping culture. On taxis, buses, in convenience stores, at restaurants — the price you see is the price you pay. No rounding up, no service charge addition, no expectation of extra cash. This applies to every transaction where you might use your T-money card or pay otherwise.\n\n## Registering your card: worth it or not?\n\nBoth T-money and Cashbee offer an optional registration process tied to a Korean phone number, which enables balance recovery if the card is lost and access to usage history through an app. For the vast majority of short-term visitors, registration isn’t worth pursuing — it typically requires a Korean phone number and sometimes a Korean bank-linked verification step that isn’t practical to set up for a one- or two-week trip.\n\nThe unregistered tourist approach (buy, load, use, refund or keep) works fine for the overwhelming majority of visits and is the path this guide assumes throughout. If you are relocating to Korea longer-term or visiting very frequently, registration becomes worth the setup effort, but that’s a different use case from the typical Busan trip.\n\n## T-money for families and groups: one card each or shared?\n\nFamilies and groups traveling together sometimes ask whether one T-money card can cover multiple people. The answer is no in any practical sense: fare gates require one tap per person passing through, and buses similarly require a tap for each rider — a single card cannot be tapped twice in quick succession for two people walking through together, as the gate will simply reject the second attempt within its cooldown window.\n\nEach traveller, including children old enough to need their own fare (younger children below a certain height typically ride free, verify current thresholds at a station), needs their own card. The practical approach for a family of four: buy four cards on arrival, load each with a similar amount, and keep them distributed so no single lost card strands the whole group without transit access.\n\n## Common card problems and how to fix them on the spot\n\n**Card not reading at the fare gate**: try a different reader position — some gates read better with the card flat against the sensor rather than at an angle. If it still fails, the card may be damaged (cracked chip from being bent in a wallet) or genuinely empty; check the balance at the nearest station reader or convenience store counter. **Insufficient balance error mid-journey**: this shows at the exit gate rather than the entry gate in most cases, since fares are calculated on exit based on distance travelled; top up at the station’s fare machine before you can exit, using the “fare adjustment” or “insufficient fare” function on the English-language menu.\n\n**Lost card**: nothing to be done for an unregistered card’s balance — treat the loss as a small, absorbed cost (typically under $10) and buy a replacement at the next convenience store. **Card works on metro but rejected on a specific bus**: rare, but some older buses have malfunctioning readers; try tapping again, and if it consistently fails, ask the driver — most carry a manual override for genuine reader faults.\n\n## Traveling beyond Busan with the same card\n\nIf your trip includes a day trip to [Gyeongju](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/gyeongju-day-trip/), [Ulsan](https://www.busan-trip.com/guides/ulsan-day-trip/), or a KTX ride onward to Seoul, the same T-money card follows you. Intercity and city buses across most of Korea accept T-money, and it works on Seoul’s subway from the moment you step off the train — no need to buy a separate card if you’re continuing your trip elsewhere in the country. Cashbee has narrower acceptance outside the Busan region, which is the main reason to default to T-money if your itinerary extends beyond the city.\n\n## Popular Busan tours\n\nVerified deep-linked tours.
